This visual pattern corresponds to the statistical finding of 24/24 forward-direction and 0/24 reverse-direction FDR-significant pairs.}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{High contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, 3 seeds).}\nDirectional asymmetry is robustly confirmed across three independent seeds (permutation Granger: 19/24 forward pairs FDR-significant, 0/24 reverse).\nHowever, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds 42, 43, 44), placing $\\alpha{=}0.75$ squarely in the transitional detection regime.\nDifferenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions at each seed, confirming that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics, a seed-level demonstration of Pitfall~1 (\\S\\ref{sec:pitfalls}).\n\n\\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}\nECE performs equivalently to token entropy across four of five methods (naive, differenced, threshold, permutation Granger).\nIts classification as ``secondary'' is driven entirely by Toda--Yamamoto Type~II errors at $T{=}10$, where bootstrap power analysis yields estimated power of 0.05--0.20 depending on $d_{\\max}$.\nResolving ECE's true canary status requires longer time series.\n\n\\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}\nNot all metrics serve as canaries.\nEffective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.\nEffective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ it \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%).\nThe GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.\nThis opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}\nOf 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs tested at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \\textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).\nThe 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).\nThe consensus framework reduces the naive 100\\% significance rate to the 37.5\\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:consensus_validation}\n\nWe validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (three seeds, 24 total pairs), no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nIndividual-method FPR ranges from \\textbf{100\\%} (naive cross-correlation) through 62.5\\% (threshold) and 37.5\\% (differenced) down to \\textbf{0\\%} (permutation Granger: 0/24 across all three seeds).\nThe consensus framework at $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves \\textbf{FPR${=}$0\\%} (0/24 pairs, three independent seeds), matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\\geq}3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\\geq}1$ generation (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nFNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\\textbf{FNR${=}$0\\%}).\nToda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\\% (2/6), missing ECE$\\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension (FPR: 3~seeds at $\\alpha{=}0.0$; FNR: 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), with cross-seed FPR variance negligible (all three seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$).\nTable~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}\nTo quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nPermutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.\nToda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.\nWe note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\\beta{=}0.3$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{False positive and false negative rates for individual methods and the consensus framework (Pythia-410M). The consensus threshold $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\%, matching the best individual method (permutation Granger) while providing robustness against miscalibration. FPR: $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (three seeds, $M{=}5$ methods); values are 3-seed means with ranges in parentheses where cross-seed variance is non-negligible. FNR: $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation, 4~seeds).}\n\\label{tab:fpr_fnr}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nCriterion & FPR ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) & FNR ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr              & 100\\%              & 0\\%  \\\\\nDifferenced XCorr        & 37.5\\%             & 0\\%  \\\\\nThreshold Onset          & 29.2\\% (12.5--62.5) & 0\\%  \\\\\nPermutation Granger      & 0\\%                & 0\\%  \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto           & 4.2\\% (0--12.5)    & 33.3\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$)              & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\nConsensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) & 0\\%    & 0\\%  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture and Cross-Domain Generalization}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nThe methodological pitfalls and consensus framework developed above are calibrated on a single model--dataset pair (Pythia-410M, WikiText-103).\nTo test whether these findings transfer across model families and data domains, we evaluate the consensus pipeline on variations in model scale, architecture family, and training domain.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Generalization of lead-lag detectability across model scale, architecture, and domain ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, single seed). 1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
```text
2	
3	\paragraph{Granger precedence is not causation.}
4	Granger causality tests whether past values of one series improve prediction of another beyond its own history; it does not establish true causation~\cite{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}.
5	Bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders: an unobserved process (such as a latent distributional shift in the training data) could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations even when no direct relationship exists.
6	Our multi-method consensus framework inherits this limitation in full.
7	Throughout this paper, ``Granger precedence'' denotes predictive improvement in a time-series forecasting sense, not a causal mechanism.
8	This distinction is why we scope our central claim to ``token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse'' rather than asserting that token entropy \emph{causes} diversity collapse.
9	
10	\paragraph{Calibration scope and generalization.}
11	All FPR and FNR calibration results reported in this work are specific to our experimental setup: Pythia-410M~\cite{biderman2023pythia}, a WikiText-103 subset, $T{=}10$ generation cycles, and five contamination levels ($\alpha \in \{0.0, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.0\}$).
12	Generalization to other model architectures, datasets, or training protocols requires independent validation.
13	The consensus framework's structure (multi-method agreement combined with multi-seed replication) is intended to be applicable beyond this specific setting, but the operating thresholds ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds) are empirically calibrated and may require re-tuning under different conditions.
14	For instance, architectures with different tokenizer granularity or training dynamics could shift the balance between false positive and false negative rates at these thresholds.
15	Initial cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (single seed, $\alpha{=}1.0$) replicates the entropy-leads-diversity pattern at Tier~3 consensus: all 6 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\geq}4/5$ method agreement (permutation Granger $p_{\text{FDR}}{=}0.003$), with 0/6 reverse-direction pairs significant.
16	Full-panel inter-method agreement remains low (Fleiss' $\kappa{=}0.091$) but rises to $\kappa{=}0.667$ among the three informative methods (\S\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), confirming that the consensus framework's structure transfers across architectures even if operating thresholds require architecture-specific calibration.
17	At $\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\geq}3/5$), placing it in the same null/low-detectability regime as Pythia (0/8 Tier~3) and confirming that the detectability boundary is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.
18	The full GPT-2-medium drift profile at $\alpha{=}0.25$ is: entropy $-32.8\%$, ECE $+422\%$, perplexity $-44.5\%$, distinct-1 $-23.5\%$, distinct-2 $-31.6\%$, distinct-3 $-30.2\%$, MAUVE $0.0315 \to 0.0282$ ($-10.5\%$).
19	Compared to Pythia ($-4.7{\pm}0.5\%$ entropy, $-40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ distinct-1), GPT-2-medium shows substantially stronger canary drift but milder diversity decline, likely due to its $8\times$ larger batch size exposing more data per step.
20	The stronger canary signal approaches detectability (permutation Granger $F{=}64.09$, $p{=}0.081$ for entropy$\to$distinct-1, compared to $p{>}0.5$ in Pythia), suggesting that the regime boundary may shift with increased effective sample size, but single-seed $T{=}10$ power remains insufficient for Confirmatory claims.
21	Toda--Yamamoto Granger finds 1/8 pairs significant, consistent with its known instability at short series lengths (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
22	
23	\paragraph{Why $K{=}4/5$ is the consensus tipping point.}
24	The $K{=}4/5$ threshold reflects the composition of the five-method panel rather than an arbitrary choice.
25	At $K{=}3/5$, the two high-FPR methods (naive cross-correlation at 100\% and differenced cross-correlation at 37.5\%) can form a majority with any single additional method, allowing false positives to pass even when both conservative methods (permutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto) disagree.
26	At $K{=}4/5$, any agreeing coalition must include at least one low-FPR method, which effectively vetoes spurious signals: null-control FPR drops from 37.5\% to 0\%.
27	Raising the threshold to $K{=}5/5$ requires Toda--Yamamoto agreement, but its low power at $T{=}10$ produces Type~II errors on 3/6 reference pairs (FNR${=}$50\%).
28	The $K{=}4/5$ threshold thus occupies the unique operating point where exactly one low-power method can be excluded without admitting high-FPR coalitions.
29	
30	\paragraph{Short time-series constraint.}
31	With $T{=}10$ generation cycles (11 data points per series), our time-series analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.
32	This series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.
33	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure's elevated FNR (33.3\%, missing 2 of 6 directional pairs) is attributable to insufficient residual degrees of freedom at this series length, not to a fundamental deficiency in the method itself.
34	This finding aligns with \citet{boettiger2012limits}, who demonstrated that early-warning signals have limited detection power in short time series.
35	Longer generation chains would improve power for all methods but impose substantial computational cost; this trade-off between statistical power and compute budget is inherent to iterative training research and cannot be resolved by methodological improvements alone.
36	However, observation window sensitivity analysis provides reassurance: the $4/5$ consensus classification stabilizes at $T{\geq}5$, well below our observation window of $T{=}10$, with identical results from $T{=}5$ through $T{=}10$ (Appendix~\ref{app:window_sensitivity}).
37	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \in \{5, \ldots, 10\}$), but with unstable sensitivity (3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length), confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.
38	
39	\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}
40	Effective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.
41	In Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.
42	In GPT-2-medium at $\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\%).
43	The likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.
44	This finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.
45	We note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.
46	
47	\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}
48	Diversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\alpha$ spectrum.
49	Diversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\pm}0.9\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\alpha$ ($+51.7\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
50	This dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.
51	
52	\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}
53	The canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.
54	At $\alpha{\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse, but at these contamination levels degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.
55	At $\alpha{\leq}0.25$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested; at $\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8), marking the onset boundary where ECE outperforms token entropy as the more sensitive canary.
56	This sensitivity difference reflects how each metric aggregates distributional information: ECE decomposes calibration error across multiple probability bins, making it responsive to changes in the shape of the predicted distribution rather than just its mean, while token entropy compresses the full distribution into a single scalar average.
57	At moderate contamination ($\alpha{=}0.50$), the distributional shifts are subtle enough that bin-level decomposition detects them but the global average does not.
58	This is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\cite{boettiger2012limits}.
59	The practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards that track cumulative trend shifts across many generations than to single-point threshold alarms.
60	If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.
61	
62	\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}
63	In the Confirmatory regime ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), the consensus framework can serve as a continuous monitoring signal within iterative training pipelines: the multi-method panel would run after each generation, and sustained Tier~3 agreement across metric pairs would flag potential collapse onset for human review.
64	This complements the continuous monitoring dashboard framing above, where cumulative trend shifts across generations provide more reliable detection than single-point threshold alarms.
65	The cost of running five lightweight statistical tests per generation is negligible relative to the cost of training itself, though the practical utility paradox (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) and the absence of intervention experiments (\S\ref{sec:limitations}) mean that such monitoring currently provides diagnostic evidence rather than actionable triggers.
66	
67	\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}
68	The finding that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity while perplexity does not ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$ after differencing) requires clarification, since perplexity is the exponential of cross-entropy and appears mathematically related to token entropy.
69	The distinction lies in the evaluation domain: our ``token entropy'' is $H(p_\theta | \text{context})$ evaluated on \emph{held-out real data} (the model's predictive uncertainty on genuine text), while ``perplexity'' is $\exp(H_\text{cross})$ evaluated on \emph{model-generated text} (the model's self-consistency on its own outputs).
70	The identity $\text{ppl} = \exp(\text{entropy})$ holds only when the model distribution equals the data distribution, a condition that iterative collapse systematically violates.
71	In practice, both metrics trend downward during collapse (the model becomes more certain), producing high undifferenced correlation.
72	First-differencing removes this shared trend: the canary$\to$diversity channel survives because entropy \emph{leads} diversity decline by one generation (the model narrows its distribution before output diversity measurably falls), whereas the canary$\to$perplexity channel vanishes because entropy and perplexity co-move \emph{synchronously}, both driven by the same underlying distributional contraction.
73	Thus, the perplexity channel being ``spurious'' is not trivially expected from the mathematical relationship, but rather a substantive finding about collapse dynamics: the generation-evaluation divergence breaks the entropy--perplexity coupling in a temporally informative way.
74	
75	\paragraph{NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis.}
76	Our application of Granger-type methods to iterative training faces constraints absent in their original econometric settings.
77	First, observation length: typical econometric applications use $T{=}100$--$1000$ time points, whereas iterative training studies are limited to $T{=}5$--$20$ generations due to computational cost.
78	This severely constrains degrees of freedom for VAR-based methods and explains the Toda--Yamamoto procedure's poor power in our setting.
79	Second, non-stationarity is by design: each generation's training data differs from the previous generation's, making the underlying process non-stationary by construction rather than as an incidental property.
80	Standard stationarity tests (ADF, KPSS) may yield misleading results when applied to processes with deterministic degradation trajectories rather than stochastic unit roots.
81	Third, the sample-size bottleneck compounds with multiple testing: with only 8--32 pairs per regime, FDR correction operates on small multiplicity, limiting its ability to separate true from false discoveries.
82	These constraints motivate our multi-method consensus approach as a complement to, rather than replacement for, traditional significance testing.
83	
84	\paragraph{Future work.}
85	Several directions extend this work.
86	Multivariate VAR models would enable testing for confounders and multi-directional Granger relationships beyond our current bivariate framework.
87	Cross-architecture validation beyond our single-seed GPT-2-medium validation requires multi-seed replication; evaluation on larger-scale models would further test generalizability.
88	Online monitoring, where canary metrics are computed within training pipelines for real-time collapse detection, is a natural applied extension.
89	Accumulate-mode experiments~\cite{dey2024breaking} would test whether canary dynamics differ from the replace-mode setting studied here.
90	Finally, connecting the empirically observed onset boundary at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ to theoretical phase transition analysis~\cite{truong2025entropy} could yield a principled explanation for why canary signals emerge at this contamination regime rather than at lower contamination levels.
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Model collapse in iterative training.}
5	
6	The phenomenon of iterative training collapse was formalized by \citet{shumailov2024curse}, who showed that recursively replacing real data with model-generated text causes progressive tail erosion across VAEs, GMMs, and language models, and by \citet{alemohammad2024mad}, who demonstrated quality--diversity tradeoffs in self-consuming generative loops for image models.
7	Theoretical analyses have since sharpened these results: \citet{dohmatob2024tale} recast collapse as a change in scaling-law exponents, showing that even small fractions of synthetic data alter effective scaling behavior, while \citet{dohmatob2025strong} proved that collapse can occur even when synthetic data constitutes a vanishingly small fraction of the training set.
8	\citet{hataya2023will} provided early empirical evidence that large-scale generative models degrade future datasets through web-scale contamination, showing that even state-of-the-art models produce training-data artifacts detectable in downstream models, a finding that motivates our focus on contamination-level-dependent analysis.
9	\citet{seddik2024bad} formalized the statistical analysis of training on synthetic data, deriving excess risk bounds that quantify how synthetic fraction and model misspecification jointly govern degradation; their framework predicts regime-dependent behavior consistent with the onset boundary we observe empirically at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$.
10	The role of data accumulation has been a central theme.
11	\citet{dey2024breaking} proved that accumulating data across generations (rather than replacing it) prevents divergence of population risk in GMMs, HMMs, and language models.
12	\citet{kazdan2025collapse} systematically compared replace and accumulate regimes, finding that synthetic data value depends on the absolute quantity of co-present real data.
13	\citet{fu2025selfconsuming} provided generalization bounds for self-consuming loops, establishing that a constant fraction of real data suffices for convergence under accumulation.
14	On the calibration side, \citet{he2025golden} showed that optimal real-data weighting converges to the golden-ratio inverse ($1/\varphi \approx 0.618$) in Gaussian and GLM settings, and that the risk landscape is non-monotone in mixing ratio, a theoretical prediction with implications for regime-dependent canary behavior.
15	Beyond distributional statistics, \citet{guo2024linguistic} documented progressive decline in lexical, syntactic, and semantic diversity across iterative fine-tuning generations, providing the multi-level diversity metrics that serve as downstream targets in our lead-lag analysis.
16	\citet{schaeffer2025position} catalogued eight distinct and sometimes contradictory definitions of ``model collapse'' in the literature, underscoring the importance of explicit operational definitions, a concern our framework addresses by scoping canary prediction to diversity metrics specifically.
17	
18	\paragraph{Canary metrics and collapse monitoring.}
19	
20	Several recent works have proposed monitoring metrics that precede or accompany distributional degradation during iterative training.
21	\citet{du2026geometric} observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models, interpreting this through a geometric-regulation lens.
22	\citet{du2025scales} report a similar pattern across model scales and mixing ratios, noting that ECE increases of 75\% appear at 30\% synthetic contamination before accuracy drops become visible.
23	Beyond calibration, SIGMA~\cite{sigma2026spectral} introduces a dual-track spectral framework based on embedding Gram matrices, where divergence between deterministic and stochastic spectral bounds serves as an early collapse diagnostic.
24	\citet{kalinowski2026topology} proposes a topological monitoring approach using persistent homology features to detect multi-scale structural loss in representation space.
25	DCES~\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal and reports improvements in ECE and entropy preservation.
26	\citet{gambetta2024surplexity} introduces surplexity, a surprise-based metric that simultaneously detects collapse and filters training data, demonstrating that high-surplexity selection matches human-data baselines in maintaining distributional balance.
27	
28	These works collectively demonstrate growing interest in collapse monitoring, yet they share methodological limitations that weaken their lead-lag claims.
29	None apply differencing tests to distinguish genuine temporal precedence from spurious co-trending; none cross-validate findings across multiple statistical methods; and none apply false discovery rate correction when testing multiple metric pairs.
30	Our work differs from the closest predecessor, \citet{du2026geometric}, in four respects:
31	(i)~we compare an entire family of canary metrics rather than a single metric pair;
32	(ii)~we cross-validate each lead-lag relationship across five independent statistical methods, quantifying inter-method sensitivity via Cohen's $\kappa$ and intra-method sensitivity via the Toda--Yamamoto augmentation order $d_{\max}$;
33	(iii)~we map detectability as a function of contamination regime rather than reporting a single aggregate result; and
34	(iv)~we treat method-selection sensitivity itself as a primary finding, showing that it is the dominant source of variation in existing lead-lag conclusions.
35	
36	\paragraph{Training dynamics monitoring.}
37	
38	A broader literature tracks within-training diagnostics such as loss of plasticity~\cite{dohare2024plasticity,lyle2023plasticity}, effective representation rank, and gradient norm statistics as indicators of learning health.
39	These approaches monitor a single training run and detect pathologies (saturated units, rank collapse, unstable gradients) that emerge over optimization steps.
40	Our work differs in a key structural way: we analyze \emph{cross-generation} temporal relationships between aggregate statistics computed after each full training cycle, applying time-series causal methods to a sequence of $T{=}10$ generation-level observations rather than tracking step-level dynamics within a single run.
41	This distinction is methodologically consequential because the short, non-stationary series that arise from generational measurement require entirely different statistical tools (Granger causality, permutation tests, differencing diagnostics) than the long, dense traces available from step-level monitoring.
42	
43	\paragraph{Early-warning signals and critical transitions.}
44	
45	The theoretical foundation for canary-based prediction of system failure originates in ecology and climate science.
46	\citet{scheffer2009early} established that complex systems approaching tipping points exhibit generic early-warning signals (rising autocorrelation and variance due to critical slowing down), independent of system-specific mechanisms.
47	\citet{scheffer2012anticipating} extended this framework by combining empirical warning indicators with network-architecture analysis, arguing that structural and statistical signals together improve anticipatory power.
48	Crucially, \citet{boettiger2012limits} quantified the statistical detection limits of these signals, showing that early-warning power is often negligible in short time series, a constraint directly relevant to iterative training, where the number of generations (typically 5--20) is far smaller than typical ecological datasets.
49	\citet{truong2025entropy} connects entropy collapse to critical transitions, characterizing it as a first-order phase transition with universality classes that determine whether classical early-warning signals retain predictive power.
50	The model collapse literature has not yet imported these methodological tools: existing canary studies neither frame their claims in terms of detection power under finite samples nor test whether observed lead-lag patterns survive the short-series constraints that \citet{boettiger2012limits} identified as fundamental.
51	
52	\paragraph{Lead-lag methodology.}
53	
54	Granger causality~\cite{granger1969investigating} provides the standard econometric framework for testing whether one time series improves prediction of another beyond its own history.
55	\citet{shojaie2022granger} review classical and modern extensions, including VAR-based tests, kernel methods, and neural approaches for nonlinear dependencies \cite{marcinkevics2022neural}.
56	A key practical issue is non-stationarity: standard Granger tests applied to integrated (unit-root) series produce inflated rejection rates, the spurious regression problem formalized by \citet{granger1974spurious}, who demonstrated that regressions between independent random walks yield spuriously significant $t$-statistics, a hazard directly applicable to co-trending metrics in iterative training.
57	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure~\cite{toda1995statistical} addresses this by augmenting the VAR lag order by the integration order $d_{\max}$ and testing only the original lags, yielding valid asymptotic inference regardless of cointegration structure; however, this robustness comes at the cost of reduced degrees of freedom, a trade-off that becomes severe in the short series ($T{\leq}20$) typical of iterative training studies.
58	Despite wide use in economics and neuroscience, these methods are largely absent from the machine learning literature on iterative training.
59	Existing canary studies rely on visual inspection or naive cross-correlation, which cannot distinguish genuine temporal precedence from shared non-stationary trends.
60	Our framework applies five complementary methods (naive cross-correlation, differenced cross-correlation, threshold onset detection, permutation Granger causality, and Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality) and requires multi-method consensus before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust, directly addressing the method-sensitivity problem that arises when any single test is taken in isolation.
61	Recent work on causal method reliability reinforces this multi-method rationale.
62	\citet{ruiz2026causalaudit} propose Causal-Audit, a pre-screening framework that validates causal assumptions before method selection and achieves 62\% false positive reduction; whereas their approach filters methods \emph{ex ante}, our consensus framework validates conclusions \emph{ex post} across methods already applied.
63	\citet{yi2026causalcompass} benchmark causal discovery robustness under eight misspecification scenarios and confirm that no single method dominates universally, directly supporting the multi-method rationale of our consensus framework.
64	Independently, \citet{sahoo2026calibration} show that calibration metrics are sensitive to training-distribution perturbation beyond the iterative collapse setting, suggesting that ECE-based canary signals may be confounded by non-collapse sources of calibration drift.
65	\citet{falahati2026curated} prove that pluralistic curation of synthetic data can prevent iterative collapse entirely, implying that canary detection is most relevant in uncontrolled data pipelines where such curation is absent.
66	
67	In summary, the model collapse literature has established the phenomenon and proposed monitoring metrics, while the econometric literature provides the statistical tools for temporal precedence testing.
68	However, no prior work bridges these two bodies of literature with the methodological rigor that short, non-stationary time series demand: systematic multi-method cross-validation, differencing diagnostics, false discovery correction, and regime-dependent calibration.
69	Our consensus framework fills this gap.
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Lead-lag analysis in iterative training collapse is far more fragile than the current literature assumes.
5	Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\%, first-differencing exposes canary$\to$perplexity associations as entirely spurious, and regime dependence causes the same Granger test to shift from 24/24 to 0/8 significant pairs across contamination levels.
6	These pitfalls establish that single-method, single-regime analyses (the current default) cannot reliably distinguish genuine temporal precedence from statistical artifacts.
7	
8	Our multi-method consensus framework addresses this fragility by requiring convergent evidence across multiple independent methods and random seeds.
9	Calibrated on effect-size reference pairs with a non-circular 4-method panel, the framework achieves FPR${=}$0\% (0/24, three null-control seeds) and FNR${=}$0\% with permutation Granger power${=}1.0$, and four tiers of practical guidance scaling from exploratory screening to publication-grade claims.
10	
11	Applying the framework, token entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, Tier~3 across 4~seeds and GPT-2-medium replication) with a consistent one-generation lead; at $\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry is confirmed (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse across 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), mapping the detectability boundary.
12	This finding is deliberately scoped to lexical diversity, not model collapse broadly, since the perplexity channel proved entirely spurious under differencing.
13	
14	The methodological pitfalls we expose arise from generic properties of short, non-stationary time series, and we conjecture the consensus framework transfers to other iterative training settings where multiple detection methods can be applied in parallel.
15	Limitations are detailed in \S\ref{sec:limitations}; future directions are discussed in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Several limitations constrain the generalizability of our findings.
5	
6	\paragraph{Model scale.}
7	Our primary experiments use Pythia-410M (410M parameters); cross-architecture validation (single-seed GPT-2-medium, 345M parameters).
8	Both are decoder-only transformers of similar scale, small by contemporary standards.
9	Whether the entropy-leads-diversity pattern and the consensus framework's operating thresholds hold at billion-parameter scale remains untested.
10	Encoder-decoder architectures (T5, BART) and non-autoregressive models (e.g., diffusion-based text generators) are entirely untested; their fundamentally different generation mechanisms may alter canary dynamics in ways that autoregressive decoder-only results cannot predict.
11	The batch size difference between architectures (4 for Pythia vs.\ 32 for GPT-2-medium) means each training step exposes $8\times$ more data in the latter, potentially accelerating collapse dynamics without altering the qualitative pattern.
12	
13	\paragraph{Data domain and scale.}
14	Training data is drawn from a 5K-document subset of WikiText-103, three orders of magnitude smaller than typical pre-training corpora.
15	This scale gap means our iterative training dynamics reflect a low-data regime where overfitting to synthetic content may onset faster than in production-scale pipelines.
16	Domain-specific properties (vocabulary richness, syntactic complexity, topical diversity) may further influence canary-downstream coupling, and domains with inherently low diversity (e.g., legal text, code) could exhibit different canary dynamics.
17	The C4-domain generalization experiments (\S\ref{sec:generalization}) introduce an additional confound: switching from WikiText to C4 simultaneously changes both the textual domain and the data-scale characteristics, preventing full disentanglement of domain effects from scale effects within a single experimental comparison.
18	
19	\paragraph{Pre-training data confound (C4).}
20	Our C4-domain generalization experiments (\S\ref{sec:generalization}) use Pythia models that were pre-trained on the Pile~\cite{gao2021pile}, which includes Common Crawl data overlapping with C4.
21	This means the model has prior exposure to the domain used for iterative training, potentially conflating domain familiarity with collapse dynamics.
22	The WikiText-103 experiments do not share this confound because Pythia's pre-training corpus does not include WikiText-103 directly.
23	Interpreting C4 results therefore requires caution: observed differences from the WikiText baseline may reflect domain familiarity effects rather than genuine domain-dependent collapse behavior.
24	
25	\paragraph{Short time series ($T{=}10$).}
26	With 11 data points per series, our statistical analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.
27	This limits power for all methods and makes the Toda--Yamamoto procedure particularly unreliable (power estimated at 0.05--0.20 for our effect sizes).
28	We omit transfer entropy, a standard nonlinear Granger alternative, because kernel density estimation requires substantially longer time series (typically $T{\geq}100$) than our 10-generation design permits, yielding negligible statistical power at $T{=}10$.
29	Longer generation chains would improve statistical power but impose substantial computational cost.
30	
31	\paragraph{Bivariate Granger $\neq$ causality.}
32	Bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders.
33	An unobserved process (such as a latent shift in the training data distribution) could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations.
34	Our multi-method consensus framework mitigates but does not eliminate this limitation; multivariate VAR models would be required to test for confounders directly.
35	
36	\paragraph{Regime boundary uncertainty.}
37	The onset boundary at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ is characterized with limited resolution (five $\alpha$ levels).
38	Whether this boundary is sharp (phase-transition-like) or gradual, and whether it shifts with model scale or data properties, remains open.
39	Three-seed replication at $\alpha{=}0.75$ confirms directional asymmetry (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), leaving the exact boundary position uncertain.
40	
41	\paragraph{No intervention experiment.}
42	We observe that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse but do not test whether acting on canary signals (e.g., halting training or reducing $\alpha$) can prevent or delay collapse onset.
43	Without intervention evidence, the practical value of the canary remains theoretical.
44	
45	\paragraph{Replace-only protocol.}
46	Our experiments use replace-only data accumulation; accumulate-mode training~\cite{dey2024breaking,kazdan2025collapse} may exhibit qualitatively different canary dynamics or eliminate the need for canary monitoring entirely~\cite{falahati2026curated}.
47	
48	\paragraph{Scope limited to diversity metrics.}
49	Our canary claims are scoped to distinct-$n$ diversity metrics~\cite{li2016diversity}, not to model collapse broadly~\cite{schaeffer2025position}, as the perplexity channel proved spurious after differencing.
50	This scoping is theoretically motivated by the calibration-diversity tradeoff~\cite{cao2025entropycal}, but means our findings may not generalize to other collapse manifestations (e.g., factual degradation, reasoning capability loss).
51	
```

potentially accelerating collapse dynamics without altering the qualitative pattern.\n\n\\paragraph{Data domain and scale.}\nTraining data is drawn from a 5K-document subset of WikiText-103, three orders of magnitude smaller than typical pre-training corpora.\nThis scale gap means our iterative training dynamics reflect a low-data regime where overfitting to synthetic content may onset faster than in production-scale pipelines.\nDomain-specific properties (vocabulary richness, syntactic complexity, topical diversity) may further influence canary-downstream coupling, and domains with inherently low diversity (e.g., legal text, code) could exhibit different canary dynamics.\nThe C4-domain generalization experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}) introduce an additional confound: switching from WikiText to C4 simultaneously changes both the textual domain and the data-scale characteristics, preventing full disentanglement of domain effects from scale effects within a single experimental comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training data confound (C4).}\nOur C4-domain generalization experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}) use Pythia models that were pre-trained on the Pile~\\cite{gao2021pile}, which includes Common Crawl data overlapping with C4.\nThis means the model has prior exposure to the domain used for iterative training, potentially conflating domain familiarity with collapse dynamics.\nThe WikiText-103 experiments do not share this confound because Pythia's pre-training corpus does not include WikiText-103 directly.\nInterpreting C4 results therefore requires caution: observed differences from the WikiText baseline may reflect domain familiarity effects rather than genuine domain-dependent collapse behavior.\n\n\\paragraph{Short time series ($T{=}10$).}\nWith 11 data points per series, our statistical analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.\nThis limits power for all methods and makes the Toda--Yamamoto procedure particularly unreliable (power estimated at 0.05--0.20 for our effect sizes).\nWe omit transfer entropy, a standard nonlinear Granger alternative, because kernel density estimation requires substantially longer time series (typically $T{\\geq}100$) than our 10-generation design permits, yielding negligible statistical power at $T{=}10$.\nLonger generation chains would improve statistical power but impose substantial computational cost.\n\n\\paragraph{Bivariate Granger $\\neq$ causality.}\nBivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders.\nAn unobserved process (such as a latent shift in the training data distribution) could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations.\nOur multi-method consensus framework mitigates but does not eliminate this limitation; multivariate VAR models would be required to test for confounders directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Regime boundary uncertainty.}\nThe onset boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ is characterized with limited resolution (five $\\alpha$ levels).\nWhether this boundary is sharp (phase-transition-like) or gradual, and whether it shifts with model scale or data properties, remains open.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ confirms directional asymmetry (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), leaving the exact boundary position uncertain.\n\n\\paragraph{No intervention experiment.}\nWe observe that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse but do not test whether acting on canary signals (e.g., halting training or reducing $\\alpha$) can prevent or delay collapse onset.\nWithout intervention evidence, the practical value of the canary remains theoretical.\n\n\\paragraph{Replace-only protocol.}\nOur experiments use replace-only data accumulation; accumulate-mode training~\\cite{dey2024breaking,kazdan2025collapse} may exhibit qualitatively different canary dynamics or eliminate the need for canary monitoring entirely~\\cite{falahati2026curated}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope limited to diversity metrics.}\nOur canary claims are scoped to distinct-$n$ diversity metrics~\\cite{li2016diversity}, not to model collapse broadly~\\cite{schaeffer2025position}, as the perplexity channel proved spurious after differencing.\nThis scoping is theoretically motivated by the calibration-diversity tradeoff~\\cite{cao2025entropycal}, but means our findings may not generalize to other collapse manifestations (e.g., factual degradation, reasoning capability loss).\n",
```json
      "numLines": 51,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 51
    }
  }
}
```

```text
5	All scientific claims, experimental design decisions, data analysis, and interpretations are the sole responsibility of the authors.

8	\paragraph{Responsible NLP considerations.}
9	This work studies degradation in iterative language model training.
10	We do not release models trained on synthetic data, as they exhibit reduced diversity and calibration.
11	Our findings highlight a failure mode relevant to the responsible deployment of language models: unmonitored iterative training on web-crawled data (which increasingly contains LLM outputs) may degrade model quality in ways detectable only through the multi-method framework we propose.
12	We encourage practitioners to implement canary monitoring in production training pipelines as a safeguard against silent quality degradation.
```

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Distinct-3 Results}
4	\label{app:distinct3}
5	
6	Table~\ref{tab:distinct3_drift} reports distinct-3 drift across all contamination levels.
7	Distinct-3 exhibits the largest absolute decline among diversity metrics at low contamination ($-58.9{\pm}0.9\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.25$) but attenuates at higher levels ($-30.5\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$), consistent with the diversity--calibration dissociation discussed in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
8	
9	\begin{table}[h]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\caption{Distinct-3 drift (\%, generation~0$\to$10) across contamination levels. $\alpha{=}0.00$, $\alpha{=}0.25$, $\alpha{=}0.75$: 3-seed mean${\pm}$std; $\alpha{=}1.0$: 4-seed mean${\pm}$std; $\alpha{=}0.50$: single seed.}
13	\label{tab:distinct3_drift}
14	\begin{tabular}{lc}
15	\toprule
16	$\alpha$ & Distinct-3 drift (\%) \\
17	\midrule
18	0.00 & $-4.4{\pm}0.5$ \\
19	0.25 & $-58.9{\pm}0.9$ \\
20	0.50 & $-52.4$ \\
21	0.75 & $-38.6{\pm}1.3$ \\
22	1.00 & $-30.5{\pm}4.0$ \\
23	\bottomrule
24	\end{tabular}
25	\end{table}
26	
27	\paragraph{Lead-lag analysis.}
28	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, token entropy Granger-precedes distinct-3 at the same consensus level as distinct-1 and distinct-2: $K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds (Tier~3).
29	However, the optimal lag is less stable than for distinct-1/2: at max\_lag${=}7$, the lag standard deviation across seeds exceeds 1.5 generations, compared to $<0.5$ for distinct-1/2.
30	This instability reflects bimodal lag distributions (modes at lag~1 and lag~3--4), suggesting that distinct-3 captures two temporally distinct diversity loss mechanisms: rapid n-gram homogenization (lag~1) and slower distributional narrowing (lag~3--4).
31	
32	\paragraph{Sensitivity to sequence length.}
33	Higher-order $n$-gram diversity metrics are more sensitive to generation length and random sampling variability.
34	With our 512-token generation length, distinct-3 has a smaller effective denominator (number of possible unique trigrams observed) than distinct-1 or distinct-2, amplifying stochastic fluctuations.
35	This partially explains the higher lag instability: the signal-to-noise ratio for temporal precedence is lower for distinct-3, requiring more observations (longer $T$ or more seeds) to achieve stable estimates.
36	For this reason, we report distinct-3 in this appendix rather than in the main text, where distinct-1 and distinct-2 provide cleaner evidence of the entropy-leads-diversity pattern.
37	
38	
39	\section{Observation Window Sensitivity}
40	\label{app:window_sensitivity}
41	
42	Table~\ref{tab:window_sensitivity} reports how the Tier~3 consensus classification ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods) varies as a function of the minimum observation window $T_{\min}$, where analysis uses only generations $0$ through $T_{\min}$.
43	The consensus classification stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$ (6 data points), well below our full observation window of $T{=}10$: all six effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\to$ distinct-1, distinct-2, distinct-3) reach $K{\geq}4/5$ agreement at every window length from $T_{\min}{=}5$ through $T_{\min}{=}10$, with no spurious pairs (canary$\to$perplexity) admitted.
44	
45	Toda--Yamamoto is the only method exhibiting window-dependent sensitivity: its significant-pair count ranges from 3/6 to 6/6 reference pairs depending on $T_{\min}$, reflecting insufficient residual degrees of freedom at shorter windows.
46	This instability does not affect the consensus classification because the remaining four methods provide stable $K{\geq}4/5$ agreement independently of Toda--Yamamoto's vote.
47	
48	\begin{table}[h]
49	\centering
50	\small
51	\caption{Observation window sensitivity for Tier~3 consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds). Tier~3 pairs: number of canary$\to$downstream pairs (out of 8 validated) reaching $K{\geq}4/5$ method agreement. TY pairs: number of effect-size reference pairs (out of 6) reaching Toda--Yamamoto significance. Consensus stabilizes at $T_{\min}{\geq}5$; TY remains unstable throughout.}

62	\bottomrule
```

```json
{
```
```json
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/background.tex << 'TEXEOF'\n\\section{Background}\n\\label{sec:background}\n\nThis section establishes the formal setup and notation used throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{Iterative training loop.}\nLet $M_0$ denote a pretrained language model and $\\mathcal{D}_\\text{real}$ a fixed corpus of human-written text.\nAt each generation $t \\geq 1$, the model $M_{t-1}$ generates a synthetic corpus $\\mathcal{D}_\\text{synth}^{(t-1)}$, and the next-generation model $M_t$ is fine-tuned on a mixture of $\\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\\alpha)$ fraction real data:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathcal{D}_\\text{train}^{(t)} = \\alpha \\cdot \\mathcal{D}_\\text{synth}^{(t-1)} \\;+\\; (1{-}\\alpha) \\cdot \\mathcal{D}_\\text{real}, \\quad \\alpha \\in [0, 1].\n    \\label{eq:mixing}\n\\end{equation}\nThe contamination level $\\alpha$ controls the fraction of machine-generated text in each training round.\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, this reduces to the full-replacement paradigm of \\citet{shumailov2024curse}, where each generation trains exclusively on the previous generation's output.\nIterating this process for $T$ generations yields a trajectory $M_0 \\to M_1 \\to \\cdots \\to M_T$.\n\n\\paragraph{Metric families.}\nWe distinguish two families of metrics evaluated at each generation~$t$.\n\\emph{Canary metrics} are cheap-to-compute statistics on the model's output distribution, hypothesized to change before downstream quality degrades, analogous to early-warning indicators studied in the context of critical transitions in dynamical systems \\cite{scheffer2009early}.\nWe consider two canary metrics: \\emph{token entropy}, the average next-token prediction entropy over held-out samples, and \\emph{expected calibration error} (ECE), which measures the discrepancy between predicted token probabilities and empirical correctness frequencies.\n\\emph{Downstream metrics} capture generation quality directly.\nWe use \\emph{distinct-$n$} ($n \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$), the ratio of unique $n$-grams to total $n$-grams in generated text \\cite{li2016diversity}, as a measure of lexical diversity, and \\emph{perplexity} on a held-out set as a measure of language modeling quality.\nEach metric produces a time series of $T{+}1$ observations (generations $0$ through $T$).\n\n\\paragraph{The lead-lag testing problem.}\nGiven two such time series $X = (x_0, \\ldots, x_T)$ and $Y = (y_0, \\ldots, y_T)$ (for instance, a canary metric and a downstream metric), the central question is whether changes in $X$ temporally precede changes in $Y$.\nThis requires more than measuring correlation: the test must establish \\emph{directionality} ($X \\to Y$ rather than $Y \\to X$) and account for confounders such as shared non-stationary trends that can produce spurious associations.\nThe classical framework for testing temporal predictive precedence is Granger causality \\cite{granger1969investigating}, which asks whether past values of $X$ improve prediction of $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history.\nWe note that Granger precedence tests predictive improvement, not true causality \\cite{shojaie2022granger}; this distinction is discussed further in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\paragraph{Five detection methods.}\nWe employ five lead-lag detection methods spanning a spectrum from permissive to conservative:\n(1)~\\emph{naive cross-correlation}, which computes Pearson correlation on undifferenced series at lags up to~5;\n(2)~\\emph{differenced cross-correlation}, which applies the same procedure to first-differenced series, removing shared linear trends;\n(3)~\\emph{threshold onset detection}, which compares the generation at which each metric first exceeds a predefined deviation threshold;\n(4)~\\emph{permutation Granger causality}, a non-parametric Granger test using 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \\cite{shojaie2022granger}; and\n(5)~\\emph{Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality}, an augmented VAR approach that tests Granger precedence without requiring prior differencing \\cite{toda1995statistical}.\nThese methods differ in their assumptions about stationarity, their sensitivity to shared trends, and their statistical power at short time-series lengths.\nTheir joint application forms the basis of the multi-method consensus framework developed in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\nTEXEOF",
  "account": "root"
}
```

```json
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/conclusion.tex << 'TEXEOF'\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nLead-lag analysis in iterative training collapse is far more fragile than the current literature assumes.\nMethod choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%, first-differencing exposes canary$\\to$perplexity associations as entirely spurious, and regime dependence causes the same Granger test to shift from 24/24 to 0/8 significant pairs across contamination levels.\nThese pitfalls establish that single-method, single-regime analyses (the current default) cannot reliably distinguish genuine temporal precedence from statistical artifacts.\n\nOur multi-method consensus framework addresses this fragility by requiring convergent evidence across multiple independent methods and random seeds.\nCalibrated on effect-size reference pairs with a non-circular 4-method panel, the framework achieves FPR${=}$0\\% (0/24, three null-control seeds) and FNR${=}$0\\% with permutation Granger power${=}1.0$, and four tiers of practical guidance scaling from exploratory screening to publication-grade claims.\n\nApplying the framework, token entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, Tier~3 across 4~seeds and GPT-2-medium replication) with a consistent one-generation lead; at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry is confirmed (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse across 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), mapping the detectability boundary.\nThis finding is deliberately scoped to lexical diversity, not model collapse broadly, since the perplexity channel proved entirely spurious under differencing.\n\nThe methodological pitfalls we expose arise from generic properties of short, non-stationary time series, and we conjecture the consensus framework transfers to other iterative training settings where multiple detection methods can be applied in parallel.\nLimitations are detailed in \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}; future directions are discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\nTEXEOF",
  "account": "root"
}
```

```json
{
```
```json
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/limitations.tex << 'TEXEOF'\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nSeveral limitations constrain the generalizability of our findings.\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nOur primary experiments use Pythia-410M (410M parameters); cross-architecture validation (single-seed GPT-2-medium, 345M parameters).\nBoth are decoder-only transformers of similar scale, small by contemporary standards.\nWhether the entropy-leads-diversity pattern and the consensus framework's operating thresholds hold at billion-parameter scale remains untested.\nEncoder-decoder architectures (T5, BART) and non-autoregressive models (e.g., diffusion-based text generators) are entirely untested; their fundamentally different generation mechanisms may alter canary dynamics in ways that autoregressive decoder-only results cannot predict.\nThe batch size difference between architectures (4 for Pythia vs.\\ 32 for GPT-2-medium) means each training step exposes $8\\times$ more data in the latter, potentially accelerating collapse dynamics without altering the qualitative pattern.\n\n\\paragraph{Data domain and scale.}\nTraining data is drawn from a 5K-document subset of WikiText-103, three orders of magnitude smaller than typical pre-training corpora.\nThis scale gap means our iterative training dynamics reflect a low-data regime where overfitting to synthetic content may onset faster than in production-scale pipelines.\nDomain-specific properties (vocabulary richness, syntactic complexity, topical diversity) may further influence canary-downstream coupling, and domains with inherently low diversity (e.g., legal text, code) could exhibit different canary dynamics.\nThe C4-domain generalization experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}) introduce an additional confound: switching from WikiText to C4 simultaneously changes both the textual domain and the data-scale characteristics, preventing full disentanglement of domain effects from scale effects within a single experimental comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training data confound (C4).}\nOur C4-domain generalization experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}) use Pythia models that were pre-trained on the Pile~\\cite{gao2021pile}, which includes Common Crawl data overlapping with C4.\nThis means the model has prior exposure to the domain used for iterative training, potentially conflating domain familiarity with collapse dynamics.\nThe WikiText-103 experiments do not share this confound because Pythia's pre-training corpus does not include WikiText-103 directly.\nInterpreting C4 results therefore requires caution: observed differences from the WikiText baseline may reflect domain familiarity effects rather than genuine domain-dependent collapse behavior.\n\n\\paragraph{Short time series ($T{=}10$).}\nWith 11 data points per series, our statistical analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.\nThis limits power for all methods and makes the Toda--Yamamoto procedure particularly unreliable (power estimated at 0.05--0.20 for our effect sizes).\nWe omit transfer entropy, a standard nonlinear Granger alternative, because kernel density estimation requires substantially longer time series (typically $T{\\geq}100$) than our 10-generation design permits, yielding negligible statistical power at $T{=}10$.\nLonger generation chains would improve statistical power but impose substantial computational cost.\n\n\\paragraph{Bivariate Granger $\\neq$ causality.}\nBivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders.\nAn unobserved process (such as a latent shift in the training data distribution) could independently drive both token entropy and diversity metrics, producing spurious lead-lag associations.\nOur multi-method consensus framework mitigates but does not eliminate this limitation; multivariate VAR models would be required to test for confounders directly.\n\n\\paragraph{Regime boundary uncertainty.}\nThe onset boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ is characterized with limited resolution (five $\\alpha$ levels).\nWhether this boundary is sharp (phase-transition-like) or gradual, and whether it shifts with model scale or data properties, remains open.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ confirms directional asymmetry (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3), leaving the exact boundary position uncertain.\n\n\\paragraph{No intervention experiment.}\nWe observe that token entropy Granger-precedes diversity collapse but do not test whether acting on canary signals (e.g., halting training or reducing $\\alpha$) can prevent or delay collapse onset.\nWithout intervention evidence, the practical value of the canary remains theoretical.\n\n\\paragraph{Replace-only protocol.}\nOur experiments use replace-only data accumulation; accumulate-mode training~\\cite{dey2024breaking,kazdan2025collapse} may exhibit qualitatively different canary dynamics or eliminate the need for canary monitoring entirely~\\cite{falahati2026curated}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope limited to diversity metrics.}\nOur canary claims are scoped to distinct-$n$ diversity metrics~\\cite{li2016diversity}, not to model collapse broadly~\\cite{schaeffer2025position}, as the perplexity channel proved spurious after differencing.\nThis scoping is theoretically motivated by the calibration-diversity tradeoff~\\cite{cao2025entropycal}, but means our findings may not generalize to other collapse manifestations (e.g., factual degradation, reasoning capability loss).\nTEXEOF",
  "account": "root"
}
```

```json
{
```
```json
  "server": "westd-19828",
  "command": "cat > /root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex << 'TEXEOF'\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nPractitioners monitoring iterative training pipelines face a diagnostic blind spot: the metrics most commonly used to detect distributional degradation can yield false positive rates of 62.5--100\\% when applied without proper statistical controls, yet no established protocol exists for distinguishing genuine early-warning signals from artifacts of shared non-stationary trends.\nThis blind spot is consequential because iterative training collapse, where models are unwittingly trained on output from earlier generations, is increasingly likely as machine-generated text proliferates on the web~\\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nTail modes vanish, lexical diversity narrows, and calibration deteriorates across generations.\nThese failure modes motivate the search for cheap-to-compute \\emph{canary metrics} that provide early warning, analogous to early-warning signals for critical transitions in ecological and climate systems~\\cite{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\n\nSeveral recent studies have pursued this direction: \\citet{du2026geometric} informally observe that expected calibration error rises before accuracy degrades; SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} proposes spectral indicators of distributional shift; and DCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal.\nYet the statistical methodology underlying these lead-lag claims (whether a canary metric genuinely \\emph{precedes} downstream degradation, or merely correlates with it under shared non-stationary trends) has received little scrutiny.\n\nWe identify several methodological deficiencies that pervade existing lead-lag analyses of iterative training collapse:\n(1)~no differencing tests to rule out spurious regression arising from shared non-stationary trends, a well-known hazard when correlating co-trending time series;\n(2)~no multi-method cross-validation; conclusions rest on a single statistical method whose implicit assumptions about stationarity and directionality are rarely examined;\n(3)~no multiple comparison correction (e.g., Benjamini--Hochberg FDR control), increasing the risk of false discoveries when testing many metric pairs simultaneously; and\n(4)~no regime-dependent analysis, despite the possibility that the same statistical test yields opposite conclusions at different contamination levels.\nThe closest predecessor is \\citet{du2026geometric}, who observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models.\nTheir analysis, however, is limited to a single metric pair, spans only 1--3 generations, and reports informal visual comparisons without statistical tests, leaving open the question of whether the observed lead is genuine or an artifact of shared trends.\nThe early-warning framework of \\citet{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}, widely adopted in ecology for anticipating tipping points, provides the theoretical motivation for canary-based prediction, but has not been systematically applied to iterative training collapse with the methodological rigor that short, noisy time series demand~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\n\nWe address these gaps through a systematic case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse, the progressive loss of lexical diversity in model-generated text across training generations.\nUsing Pythia-410M across five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$) and up to four random seeds, we expose three methodological pitfalls that undermine naive lead-lag analysis:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Method sensitivity.} Applied to the same data (16 candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, including candidates later excluded; \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (poor agreement).\n    \\item \\textbf{Spurious regression.} Canary$\\to$perplexity associations appear near-universal in undifferenced analysis but vanish entirely after first-differencing ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), revealing them as artifacts of co-trending rather than genuine temporal precedence.\n    \\item \\textbf{Regime dependence.} Permutation Granger tests find 24/24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (0/8--6/8, three seeds), demonstrating that detectability depends sharply on both contamination level and training stochasticity.\n\\end{enumerate}\nTo move beyond single-method analysis, we propose a multi-method consensus framework with empirically calibrated thresholds, requiring agreement across multiple independent statistical methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\nThe framework's utility is demonstrated by its calibration on built-in experimental controls.\nNaive cross-correlation identifies 100\\% of canary--collapse metric pairs as FDR-significant; the consensus pipeline (requiring ${\\geq}4/5$ methods and ${\\geq}3/4$ seeds) retains only those pairs meeting effect-size criteria calibrated against the null control.\nOn reference extremes, the framework achieves a false positive rate of 0\\% at the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (0/24 pairs, three seeds) (where individual methods reach up to 100\\% FPR) and a false negative rate of 0\\% against six effect-size reference pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nApplying the framework, we identify token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$: the full consensus pipeline confirms directional asymmetry with cross-correlation $|r|{>}0.99$, 24/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant, and 0/24 in the reverse direction.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ extends this finding (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse via permutation Granger), though method-level consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3).\nThe onset boundary of causal detectability lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, below which all seeds agree on null.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic exposure of three methodological pitfalls} (method sensitivity, spurious regression, regime dependence) in lead-lag analysis of iterative training collapse.\n    Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%; first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely; and the same Granger test shifts from 24/24 FDR-significant pairs to 0/8 across contamination levels.\n    These results demonstrate that single-method, single-regime analyses, the current norm in this literature, are unreliable.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\\%).\n    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.\n    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture and cross-domain generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): evaluation of the consensus pipeline across model scale (Pythia-1B), architecture family (OPT-1.3B), and training domain (C4), testing whether the methodological pitfalls and detectability boundaries identified on Pythia-410M / WikiText-103 transfer to other settings. \\textbf{[Results TBD.]}\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys related work on model collapse, early-warning signals, and lead-lag methodology.\n\\S\\ref{sec:background} establishes the formal setup, notation, and the five detection methods used throughout.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} distills the three methodological pitfalls into a unified diagnostic framework, presents the multi-method consensus pipeline, and reports its empirical calibration.\n\\S\\ref{sec:experiments} provides full experimental results: method sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), regime-dependent detectability (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), a case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), consensus framework validation (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}), and cross-architecture generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications (including the practical utility paradox, the token entropy--perplexity distinction, and NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis) and outlines directions for future work.\n\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes, followed by a dedicated Limitations section (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nTEXEOF",
  "account": "root"
}
```
